98 ESCLAMACIONES

...forces against Him who wages war for you <note>original: "haze la guerra." In this context, it refers to God's "war" of love to win over the soul, or perhaps the soul's own internal conflict.</note>, and who wishes to take away your birthright <term>mayorazgo: a legal term for an inherited estate that stays within a family; here, it refers to the soul’s divine inheritance of Heaven</term>. Come back, come back to your senses; open your eyes. Ask with great cries and tears for light from Him who gave light to the world. Understand, for the love of God, that you are attempting to kill with all your strength the One who lost His own life to give life to you. Look: He is the very one who defends you from your enemies. 

And if all this is not enough, let it be enough for you to know that you can do nothing against His power, and that sooner or later you must pay with eternal fire for such great disrespect and audacity. Is it because you see this Divine Majesty bound and tied by the love He has for us? What more did those who put Him to death do, other than—after He was bound—strike Him with blows and wounds? 

O my God, how You suffer for one who feels so little sorrow for Your suf- <note>The word "pe-" likely completes to "penas" (sorrows or pains) on the following page.</note>

<summary>In this passage, the author passionately pleads with the reader to cease their spiritual rebellion, reminding them that Christ’s "weakness" in the face of human sin is actually a sign of His overwhelming love.</summary>
<keywords>repentance, spiritual warfare, birthright, Passion of Christ, divine love, justice, St. Teresa of Avila</keywords>

->OF THE SOUL TO GOD. 99<-

...nesses? <note>The text begins with the end of a word from the previous page, likely "wickednesses" or "eternal things."</note> The time will come, Lord, when Your justice must be made known, and [it will be seen] if it is equal to Your mercy. Look, Christians, let us consider it well, for we shall never be able to fully understand how much we owe to our Lord God, and the magnificence of His mercies. For if His justice is so great—oh, the sorrow! oh, the sorrow!—what will become of those who have deserved that it be carried out and shine forth upon them?

->100 EXCLAMATIONS<-

...such great offenses committed against my God in these unfortunate times, and seeing such ingratitude, and seeing that people refuse to acknowledge the multitude of souls being carried away by Satan. O blessed celestial souls, help us in our misery and be our intercessors before the divine mercy, so that it may grant us some of your joy and share with us that clear knowledge which you possess. 

Grant us, my God, the understanding of what is given to those who fight courageously <note>original: "varonilmẽte" — literally "manfully," a term used in the 16th century to denote spiritual strength and fortitude regardless of gender.</note> in this dream <note>The author refers to the common Baroque theme that earthly life is a fleeting illusion compared to the reality of eternity.</note> of this miserable life. Obtain for us, O loving souls, the understanding of the joy you feel in seeing the eternity of your delights; and how delightful it is to see for certain that they shall never end. O, how wretched we are, my Lord, for we know and believe it well; but...

<summary>In this passage, the author laments the loss of souls to evil and petitions the saints in heaven to intercede for the living, seeking the strength to endure earthly life.</summary>

->FROM THE SOUL TO GOD. 101<-

...because of the great habit of not considering these truths, they are now so foreign to souls that they neither recognize them, nor do they even wish to know them!

O self-interested people, greedy for your own tastes and delights! Because you will not wait a brief time to enjoy them in such abundance—refusing to wait a year, a day, an hour, and perhaps <note>original: "por ventura," a phrase meaning "by chance" or "perhaps."</note> no more than a single moment—you lose everything just to enjoy the misery you see present before you.

Oh, oh, oh, how little we trust in You, Lord! How many greater riches and treasures You entrusted to us; for You gave us thirty-three years <note>The traditional duration of the life of Jesus Christ on Earth.</note> of great labors, and afterwards a death so intolerable and pitiful. You gave Your Son to us, and did so many years before our birth; and even knowing that we were not going to repay You, You did not wish—

### XIV.

**O** Lord and my true God, whoever does not know You, does not love You. Oh, what a great truth this is! But alas, the sorrow! Alas, the sorrow, O Lord, for those who do not wish to know You. A fearful thing is the hour of death: but alas! alas!

->FROM THE SOUL TO GOD. 103<-

My Creator, how frightening will be the day when your justice must be carried out? I often consider, my Christ, how pleasant and how delightful your eyes appear to those who love you, and whom you, my Good <note>The phrase "my Good" (Spanish: *bien mio*) is a common term of endearment in Spanish mystical literature, referring to God as the ultimate source of goodness and love.</note>, choose to look upon with love. Oh, may God help me! How poorly can this be explained, except to those who have already understood how sweet the Lord is! ->104 EXCLAMATIONS<-

...declared a pitched battle against God with all our senses and the powers of the soul <term>powers of the soul: in 16th-century theology, these were defined as the Memory, the Understanding, and the Will.</term>: he who has the most power invents the most treasons against his King.

You already know, my Lord, that many times I felt a greater fear when I remembered that I might see Your divine face angry against me on that terrifying day of the final judgment, than [the fear of] all the pains and furies of hell that were presented to me; and I pleaded with You that Your mercy would protect me from something so pitiful for me, and so I plead with You now, Lord. 

What can happen to me on earth that compares to this? I would welcome it all at once, my God, if You would deliver me from such great affliction: let me not, my God, let me not fail to enjoy such great beauty <note>Teresa of Avila often emphasizes the "beauty" of Christ's humanity as a central focus of her mystical experiences.</note> in peace. ->FROM THE SOUL TO GOD. 105<-

...grace <note>The text begins with a fragment, likely the end of a word like "grace" from the previous page.</note>: I confess, Eternal Father, that I have kept it poorly; but there is still a remedy, Lord: there is a remedy as long as we live in this exile <note>A common term in mystical writing referring to earthly life as a temporary separation from the soul's true home in Heaven.</note>.

O brothers, O brothers, and children of this God, let us take heart, let us take heart! For you know that His Majesty <note>In the context of Spanish Golden Age spirituality, "His Majesty" is a standard way of referring to God, blending religious devotion with the language of the royal court.</note> says that when we are sorry for having offended Him, He will no longer remember our faults and wicked deeds. O, mercy so beyond measure! What more could we want? Perchance is there anyone who would not be ashamed to ask for so much? Now is the time to receive what this merciful Lord and our God gives us, for He desires our friendship. Who would deny it to Him, who did not deny us the shedding of all His blood, and the loss of His life for our sake? See how little He asks; for it is to our own benefit that we do it. O, help me...

<summary>This page contains an impassioned exhortation to the reader to seek God's mercy and friendship, emphasizing that God forgets the sins of those who are truly repentant. The author reflects on the sacrifice of Christ as the ultimate proof of God's desire for reconciliation with humanity.</summary>

106 EXCLAMATIONS

O God, Lord! Oh, what hardness, oh what folly and blindness! For if a small thing is lost—a needle, or a hawk <note>original: "gauilan". In the 16th century, hawks were prized for sport; here the author contrasts a trivial lost pet with the loss of God.</note> that serves no purpose other than giving a little delight to the sight by seeing it fly through the air—it causes us grief. And yet, should we not feel grief at the loss of this mighty eagle <note>original: "aguila caudaloſa". A metaphor for the soul’s ascent or the Divine Majesty itself.</note> of the majesty of God, and a kingdom whose enjoyment shall have no end? What is this? What is this? I do not understand it. Remedy, my God, such great folly and blindness.

XV.

Woe is me! Woe is me! Lord, how very long is this exile <note>The author views earthly life as a "banishment" or exile from her true home in Heaven.</note>, and it passes with great hardships born of the desire for my God. Lord, what shall a soul do, imprisoned in this jail? <note>A traditional mystical theme: the physical body and the material world acting as a prison for the spirit.</note> Oh Jesus, how long is the life of man, even...

<summary>In this passage, the author laments the spiritual blindness of humanity, noting how people grieve over trivial worldly losses while remaining indifferent to the loss of God's kingdom. She expresses a profound, painful longing for the afterlife, describing earthly existence as a long and difficult exile.</summary>

<keywords>Exclamations, spiritual blindness, desire for God, exile of life, soul as prisoner, Teresa of Avila, mysticism</keywords>

# FROM THE SOUL TO GOD. 107

...which is said to be brief! Brief it is, my God, for winning through it a life that can never end: but very long for the soul that desires to see itself in the presence of its God. What remedy do you give for this suffering? There is none, except when it is suffered for you. O my sweet rest for the lovers of my God, do not fail the one who loves you, for through you the torment that the Beloved <note>The "Beloved" is a common mystical term for Christ, based on the Song of Solomon.</note> causes the soul who desires him must grow and be eased.

I desire, Lord, to please you: but I know well that my contentment lies in nothing mortal <note>original: "ninguno de los mortales," implying that no earthly person or thing can satisfy the soul.</note>. Since this is so, you will not blame my desire: see me here, Lord; if it is necessary to live to do you some service, I do not refuse any of the hardships on earth that may come to me, as your lover Saint Martin <note>Saint Martin of Tours (c. 316–397) was a bishop famous for his quote: "Lord, if I am still necessary to your people, I do not refuse the labor."</note> used to say.

But oh, what pain! Oh, woe is me, Lord—

<summary>The author expresses an intense spiritual longing to be united with God, viewing life as a long exile while simultaneously offering herself for any earthly hardship if it serves the divine will. She references Saint Martin as a model for someone willing to postpone their heavenly reward to serve others.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual longing, Saint Martin of Tours, divine love, suffering, eternal life, mysticism</keywords>

->110 EXCLAMATIONS<-

...reason that loves much does not admit advice or comfort, except from the very one who wounded it, because from there it hopes its pain will be remedied. When you wish, Lord, you quickly heal the wound you have given. Before that, there is no health or joy to be expected, except that which is drawn from suffering so well spent <note>Teresa describes the "sweet pain" of spiritual longing; the soul finds a strange kind of joy in the suffering caused by its desire for God.</note>.

O true lover, with what mercy, with what sweetness, with what delight, with what tenderness, and with what greatest demonstrations of love you heal these wounds, which you have made with the arrows of love itself! <note>The "arrows of love" refers to the concept of transverberation, where the soul feels physically and spiritually pierced by God's love.</note> O my God, and rest from all sorrows, how bewildered I am <note>original: "desatinada," which carries the sense of being out of one's mind or lost in a spiritual daze</note>. How could there be human means to cure those whom the divine fire has made ill? Who can know how deep this wound goes, or from where it proceeded, or how it can be [healed...]

<summary>In this passage, Saint Teresa of Avila reflects on the "divine fire" of God's love, explaining that the spiritual wounds caused by such love can only be healed by God Himself.</summary>

<keywords>Teresa of Avila, mysticism, divine love, spiritual healing, transverberation, prayer, Spanish Golden Age</keywords>

Well, if it is lowly, my Spouse <note>The author addresses God/Christ as the "Spouse" of the soul.</note>, why does it not stop at any created thing until it reaches its Creator? Oh my God, why "I to my beloved"? <meta>This page continues the spiritual reflections of the author, likely St. Teresa of Avila, focusing on the mystical union through the metaphor of battle and surrender. It follows the theme of the soul's longing for God.</meta>

->112 EXCLAMATIONS<-

Lord, once this battle has begun, against whom must they go to fight, if not against the one who has made himself master of this fortress <note>The "fortress" is a hallmark of Teresian imagery, representing the "Interior Castle" or the innermost sanctuary of the soul where God resides.</note> where they used to dwell—which is the highest part of the soul—and has cast them out, so that they might return to conquer their conqueror? And being already weary of finding themselves without him, they quickly surrender; they spend themselves, losing all their strength, and yet they fight better, and in surrendering, they conquer their conqueror. Oh, my soul, what an admirable battle you have waged in this sorrow, and how literally this comes to pass! For "my beloved is mine, and I am his" <note>original: "mi amado à mi: y yo à mi amado." This is a reference to the Song of Solomon 2:16, a biblical text frequently used by mystics to describe the intimate union between the soul and the Divine.</note>; who will be the one to step in to separate them, or to extinguish two such burning fires? It would be labor in vain, because the two have already become one.

->### FROM THE SOUL TO GOD. 113<-

->### XVII.<-

Oh my God, and my infinite Wisdom without measure and without limit, who are above all angelic and human understandings! Oh Love, you who love me more than I can love myself, or even understand! Why should I want, Lord, to desire more than what you might wish to give me? Why should I tire myself by asking you for things dictated by my own desire? For everything that my intellect can devise and my desire can long for, you have already understood their ultimate ends; yet I do not understand how to truly benefit myself. In that which my soul thinks it will find a gain, perhaps therein lies my loss. For if I ask you to free me from a trial <note>original: "trabajo," which in this context refers to a difficult labor, hardship, or suffering</note>, and within that trial lies the very purpose of my mortification <term>mortification: the spiritual practice of subduing the physical senses and self-will to achieve a higher state of religious devotion</term>, what is it that I...

114 ->EXCLAMATIONS<-

...ing, my God? <note>The page begins mid-word with "do," likely the end of a word like "hablando" (speaking) or "pidiendo" (asking) from the previous page.</note> If I beg you to give it to me <note>The "it" likely refers to a cross or a specific suffering requested in the preceding text.</note>, perhaps it is not suited to my patience, which is still weak and cannot endure such a heavy blow. And if I do endure it with patience, yet am not strong in humility, I might think that I have accomplished something myself, when it is you who does everything, my God. 

If I desire to suffer, I would prefer not to do so in matters where it seems unhelpful to your service to lose my "credit" <note>original: "credito" — meaning her reputation, standing, or the public's trust in her</note>—even if I personally do not feel the sting of dishonor. Yet it may be that through the very cause I think will lead to loss, more is gained for my true purpose, which is to serve you.

I could say many more things on this matter, Lord, to make myself understood—for I do not even understand myself! But since I know that you understand these things, why do I speak at all? I do so so that when I see my "misery" <term>Misery: A common 16th-century term for human frailty, referring to the innate weakness and tendency toward error in the human condition.</term> awakened, my God, and my reason blinded, I may...

She concludes that while she cannot fully understand her own motivations, she speaks to God as a way of acknowledging her human frailty.</summary>

116 ->EXCLAMATIONS<-

[...lest You] punish me by giving me what I want or desire, if Your love, which should live in me always, does not desire it: let this "I" <note>The author refers to the "self" or ego that must be surrendered in mystical union.</note> die now, and let another live in me who is more than I, and for me better than I, so that I may serve Him: let Him live, and give me life: let Him reign, and let me be His captive: for my soul desires no other freedom. How shall he be free who is estranged from the Most High? What greater or more miserable captivity than for the soul to be released from the hand of its Creator? Blessed are those who find themselves imprisoned with the strong shackles and chains of the benefits of God’s mercy, and rendered unable to have the power to free themselves. Love is as strong as death, and as harsh as hell. <note>original: "Fuerte es como la muerte el amor, y duro como el infierno." ->FROM THE SOUL TO GOD. 117<-

...it might hope to be able to leave, or to put it better, it might not fear seeing itself outside. But alas for me, my Lord, for as long as this mortal life lasts, eternal life is always in danger. O life, enemy of my well-being, who might have permission to end you? I endure you because God endures you; I sustain you because you are His: do not be a traitor to me, nor ungrateful. Despite all this, alas for me, my Lord, for my exile <note>original: "deſtierro"; a recurring theme in mystical literature where the soul views earthly life as a period of banishment from its true home in Heaven</note> is long. All time is brief when given in exchange for Your eternity, yet a single day, and even an hour, is very long for one who does not know and fears if they are to offend You. O free will <term>libre aluedrio: the human capacity to choose between good and evil, often described by mystics as a burden until it is aligned with God's will</term>, so enslaved by your own liberty, unless you live nailed fast <note>original: "enclauado"; this word carries the connotation of being "nailed" to a cross, suggesting the soul must be fixed in place by devotion</note> by the fear and love of Him who created you. Oh, when will that blessed day come when you shall see yourself drowned in that infinite sea of ultimate truth, where you will no longer be free to sin,

->118 EXCLAMATIONS<-

...nor will you want to be anything else, because you will be safe from all misery, naturalized within the life of your God. He is blessed, because He knows, and loves, and enjoys Himself, without any other thing being possible: He does not have, nor can He have—nor would it be part of God’s perfection to be able to have—the liberty to forget Himself, or to cease loving Himself. Then, my soul, you will enter into your rest when you become deeply entwined <note>The original Spanish *entrañares* suggests being rooted in the very core or "innards" of something.</note> with this Highest Good, and when you understand what He understands, and love what He loves, and enjoy what He enjoys. Since you will then see your changeable will lost—now, now, no more change!—because the grace of God has been so powerful that it has made you a partaker <note>original: *particionera*. This refers to the mystical concept of the soul sharing in the essence of God.</note> of His divine nature with such perfection that you no longer can, nor desire to be able to, forget the Highest Good, nor cease to enjoy Him along with His love.

->FROM THE SOUL TO GOD. 119<-

Blessed are those who are written in the book of this life. But you, my soul, if you are among them, why are you sad, and why do you trouble me? <note>The author is paraphrasing Psalm 42:5, a common rhetorical device in spiritual "soliloquies" where the writer addresses their own internal state.</note> Hope in God, for even now I will confess to Him my sins and His mercies; and from all of it together, I will make a song of praise with perpetual sighs to my Savior and my God. 

It may be that a day will come when my glory <note>original: "mi gloria." In this context, "glory" likely refers to the soul’s higher faculties or its future state in heaven.</note> sings to Him, and my conscience is no longer pricked by remorse: where all sighs and fears shall finally cease. But meanwhile, in hope and silence shall be my strength. <note>A reference to the biblical book of Isaiah 30:15: "In quietness and in confidence shall be your strength," a cornerstone verse for practitioners of contemplative prayer.</note> 

I would rather live and die striving for and hoping for eternal life, than possess all created things and all their earthly goods which must come to an end. Do not forsake me, Lord, because in You I hope: let my hope not be put to shame. Let me serve You always, and do with me what You will.

<summary>A contemplative prayer where the author encourages their soul to find strength in silent hope and the promise of eternal life rather than earthly riches. It reflects the "soliloquy" style of meditation common in 16th and 17th-century devotional literature.</summary>
<keywords>devotion, soul, hope, eternal life, silence, confession, Psalm 42, Isaiah 30, spiritual strength</keywords>

**L**and that is not tilled will bear thistles and thorns, even if it be fertile; so too is the understanding of man. <note>Teresa uses an agricultural metaphor here, common in her writing, to explain that even a naturally gifted or "fertile" mind requires spiritual discipline and "cultivation" to avoid growing harmful thoughts.</note>

When among many people, always speak little.

Be modest in all things that one may do or deal with.

Never argue much, especially in matters of little importance. <note>original: "Nunca porfiar mucho" — This suggests avoiding stubbornness or persistence in trivial debates to maintain inner peace.</note>

### ->ADVICE OF ST. TERESA. 121<-

Speak to everyone with moderate joy.

Do not make fun of anything.

Never reprimand anyone without discretion and humility, and a proper sense of one's own shortcomings. <note>original: "confuſion propria de ſi miſma." In this context, "confusión" refers to a humble awareness or holy shame regarding one's own faults before God.</note>

Adapt yourself to the temperament <term>temperament (compliſion): a 16th-century medical and psychological concept referring to a person's physical and emotional makeup</term> of the person you are dealing with: with the joyful, be joyful; and with the sad, be sad; in short, become all things to all people, so as to win them all. <note>This is a paraphrase of St. Paul’s words in 1 Corinthians 9:22, a common theme in Jesuit and Carmelite spirituality regarding "holy sociality."</note>

Never speak without thinking it through well, and commending it greatly to our Lord, so that you do not say anything that might displease Him.

Never make excuses for yourself, except in very justifiable <note>original: "prouable cauſa," meaning a case that can be proven necessary or just.</note> circumstances.

Never say anything about yourself worthy of praise, such as your knowledge, virtues, or lineage, unless there is hope that it will be of benefit; and even then, let it be with humility and the realization that these are gifts from the hand of God.

### 124 COUNSELS OF ST. TERESA

...fulfillment of obedience, as if Jesus Christ himself commanded it of you through your **prior** <term>prior: the superior or head of a religious house</term> or **prelate**. <term>prelate: a high-ranking member of the clergy, such as a bishop or abbot, who has jurisdiction over others</term>

In every work and at every hour, examine your conscience; and having seen your faults, strive for amendment with divine favor. By this path, you will achieve perfection.

Do not dwell on the faults of others, but rather on their virtues and your own faults.

Always walk with great desires to suffer for Christ in every thing and occasion. <note>original: "padecer por Christo." In the 16th-century Spanish mystical tradition, "suffering" was not merely physical pain, but the joyful acceptance of daily hardships as a way to unite one’s soul with the Passion of Jesus.</note>

Make fifty offerings of yourself to God each day; do this with great fervor and desire for God.

Keep present throughout the day that which you meditated upon in the morning; apply much diligence to this, for there is great profit <note>In this context, "profit" refers to spiritual growth and the accumulation of grace rather than material gain.</note> in it.

-> TO HIS NUNS. 125 <-

...[whatever God] gives her in prayer.

Always flee from eccentricity <note>The original Spanish 'singularidad' refers to the behavior of a monk or nun who tries to appear more holy or different from the rest of the community, which was considered a sign of pride.</note>, as much as possible: for it is a great evil for the community.

Read the ordinances and the rule of your religious order <note>The 'Rule' refers to the formal documents governing a monastic life, such as the Rule of St. Benedict or the Rule of St. Albert.</note> many times; and keep them truly.

In all created things, behold the providence and wisdom of God; and in all of them, praise Him.

Detach your heart from all things: and seek, and you will find God.

Never show devotion outwardly that does not exist within: but you may certainly hide your devotion.

Do not show your interior devotion, except out of great necessity. "My secret is mine to keep," <note>original: "Mi secreto para mi." This is a translation of the Latin 'Secretum meum mihi,' a phrase from Isaiah 24:16 frequently cited by St. Bernard of Clairvaux and St. Francis of Assisi to emphasize the private nature of mystical experience.</note> say Saint Francis and Saint Bernard.

Regarding food, whether it is well or poorly cooked, do not complain; remembering the gall and vinegar of Jesus Christ <note>A reference to the beverage offered to Jesus during His crucifixion to increase His suffering.</note>.

<meta>This page continues a series of spiritual maxims or "Avisos" (Advices) attributed to Saint Teresa of Ávila. These short, practical instructions were designed to guide the daily conduct and internal disposition of nuns within the Discalced Carmelite reform.</meta>

->126 ADVICE OF ST. TERESA<-

At the table, speak to no one, nor lift your eyes to look at another. <note>Monastic meals were typically taken in silence while listening to a spiritual reading, as a way to practice discipline and avoid the distractions of gossip.</note>

Consider the table of heaven and its food, which is God, and the guests, who are the angels; lift your eyes toward that table, desiring to see yourself there. <note>The author encourages the reader to transform the physical act of eating into a meditation on the "Eternal Banquet" of the afterlife.</note>

In the presence of your superior <term>Superior: the person in charge of the religious community, such as a Mother Superior or Prioress</term>—in whom you should see Jesus Christ—never speak except for what is necessary, and with great reverence.

Never do anything that you could not do in front of everyone.

Do not make comparisons between one person and another, because it is an odious <note>original: "odioſa"; in this context, it refers to something that breeds resentment or discord among the sisters</note> thing.

When you are reprimanded for something, receive it with interior and exterior humility; and pray to God for the one who reprimanded you.

When a superior commands one thing, do not say that another commands the opposite; instead, think that all have holy intentions, and obey what is commanded of you. <note>This highlights the importance of the vow of obedience, suggesting that the spirit of the command is more important than the potential contradictions between different leaders.</note>

->TO HER NUNS. 127<-

In matters that do not concern her <note>original: "no le va ni le viene," an idiom meaning things that are none of her business</note>, she should not be curious to speak of them or ask questions.

Keep your past life in mind to weep for it, as well as your present lukewarmness <term>lukewarmness: "tibieza," referring to a state of spiritual apathy or a lack of religious fervor</term>, and the distance yet to travel from here to heaven, so as to live with fear <note>In this context, a "holy fear" or reverential awe that keeps one from sin</note>, which is the source of great blessings.

Always do what those of the house <note>referring to the other members of the religious community</note> tell you, unless it is against obedience; and answer them with humility and gentleness.

Do not ask for any specific thing regarding food or clothing, except in cases of great necessity.

Never cease to humble yourself and practice mortification <term>mortification: "mortificarse," the practice of self-denial or physical/spiritual discipline to overcome the ego</term> in all things until death.

Always accustom yourself to performing many acts of love, because they kindle and soften the soul.

Perform acts of all the other virtues.

# TO HIS NUNS. 129

...stung <note>original: "pungida," meaning pierced by remorse or sharp spiritual feeling</note> and humbled.

Observe carefully how quickly people change, and how little they are to be trusted; and so, cling tightly to God, who does not change.

Endeavor to discuss the matters of your soul with your spiritual and learned confessor <term>confessor: a priest who hears a person's sins and provides personalized spiritual guidance</term>, to whom you should communicate everything; and follow him in all things.

Every time you receive Holy Communion, ask God for some gift, because of the great mercy with which He has come to your poor soul.

Although you may have many Saints as your advocates <note>In this tradition, "advocates" are patron saints who intercede with God on behalf of the believer</note>, let Saint Joseph be your advocate in particular: for he obtains much from God.

In times of sadness and agitation, do not abandon the good works of prayer and penance that you were accustomed to doing: for the devil seeks to disturb you so that you will give them up: instead, do more than you used to: and you will see how quickly the Lord favors you.

<meta>This page contains a set of spiritual maxims or "counsels" attributed to St. Teresa of Avila (1515–1582), a Spanish mystic and reformer of the Carmelite Order. The text is written in Early Modern Spanish with a Latin closing and a French library stamp.</meta>

130 COUNSELS OF ST. TERESA TO HER NUNS.

Do not share your temptations and imperfections with those who are the least spiritually advanced <note>The original Spanish *desaprouechadas* (modern *desaprovechadas*) literally means "unprofitable" or "unproductive," referring here to novices or those who have not yet made significant progress in their spiritual discipline.</note> in the house, for you will do harm to yourself and to the others; instead, share them with those who are the most perfect.

Remember that you have but one soul; nor must you die more than once; nor do you have more than one brief life, which is your own individual life; nor is there more than one glory, and that one is eternal; if you remember this, you will let go <note>The phrase *daràs de mano* is an idiom meaning to set aside, abandon, or stop worrying about worldly concerns.</note> of many things.

Let your desire be to see God; your fear, whether you might lose Him; your sorrow, that you do not yet enjoy Him; and your joy, in that which can lead you there; and you will live with great peace.

->THANKS BE TO GOD.<- <note>original Latin: *Deo Gratias*</note>

10      ### MEDITATIONS

...lest it <note>devotion or the soul</note> should grow lukewarm through the frequent repetition of this holy prayer <note>The Lord's Prayer, also known as the "Our Father."</note>, it would not be out of place to seek some means so that, in reciting it every day, we might always conceive new thoughts to maintain our spirit and our will in a vigor that is always new.

...the life of our soul; since out of the seven petitions contained in this holy prayer, the first three—which are the hallowing of God’s name, His kingdom, and His will—concern Him; and among the final ones which concern us, there is only this one <note>This refers to the fourth petition: "Give us this day our daily bread."</note> through which we pray to Him to give us something. THE Fifth Petition, which states, "Forgive us our debts as we forgive those who have offended us," being linked to the title of **Redeemer**, is very fitting for Friday. ->ON THE LORD’S PRAYER. 69<- <note>The original title "SVR LE PATER" refers to the *Pater Noster*, the Latin name for the "Our Father" or Lord's Prayer.</note>

...[deliver us] from the sins into which our weakness might cause us to fall in the future.

Let us consider how the physicians of this earth behave. They only go to visit those who send for them; and it is not the most severely ill whom they visit most frequently, but rather those who pay them the best. They represent the illness as being greater than it truly is, and sometimes even prolong it so that they might earn more. They treat the poor based on the reports of others, while attending to the rich in person. Furthermore, they provide the remedies they prescribe for neither the one nor the other; instead, these must be obtained from elsewhere, and often at a high price, even though the cure remains highly uncertain.

O heavenly Physician, <note>original: "Medecin"</note> you resemble these others in name only... <note>The text cuts off here, beginning a contrast between the perceived greed of 17th-century doctors and the selfless nature of divine healing.</note>

<summary>This page uses a sharp social critique of earthly doctors—accusing them of greed and favoritism—to create a contrast with the "Heavenly Physician" (God).</summary>

->SEVENTH PETITION.<-

->For Sunday.<-

*Deliver us from evil.*

**W**HEN, by this seventh and final petition, we pray to God to deliver us from evil; we do not specify in particular from which evil we ask Him to deliver us. --- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the nature of evil and sin, likely part of a commentary on the Lord's Prayer ("Deliver us from evil"). The text begins mid-word with the conclusion of the word "meditations."</meta>

->78      MEDITATIONS<-

...tations, illnesses, afflictions, the displeasures that affect honor <note>In the 17th-century context, "honor" referred to one's social reputation and standing; a "displeasure" to honor meant a public disgrace or loss of status.</note>, and other such things. But these cannot properly be called "evils," except insofar as they serve as an occasion to fall into sin; and for this same reason, riches, honors, and all temporal <term>goods<note>Worldly, physical possessions or status that are temporary, as opposed to eternal or spiritual assets.</note></term> can rightly be called "evils"; because they often also provide us an occasion to offend God. Thus we ask to be delivered, not only from all these evils; but also from all those goods which might cause us to fall into eternal condemnation. And because it properly belongs to the Sovereign Judge to free us from these penalties, the title of Judge suits God very well in this instance.

<summary>This page presents a theological argument that worldly suffering is only "evil" if it leads to sin, while worldly wealth and status can also be considered "evil" if they lead to spiritual ruin. It concludes by addressing God as the Sovereign Judge who delivers the soul from these spiritual dangers.</summary>
<keywords>temporal goods, sin, honor, Sovereign Judge, eternal condemnation, spiritual trial, deliverance</keywords>

->88      MEDITATIONS<-

...in such a great solitude? What can you do, since everything you do is imperfect and flawed? O my soul, who can comfort you, seeing you exposed upon a sea so full of storms and tempests? I grieve <note>The original French *m'afflige* implies a deep spiritual distress or mourning.</note> when I consider myself as I am; and I am even more grieved to have lived for so long without being grieved. <note>The author employs a paradox common in mystical literature: the "affliction" of the soul is actually a sign of spiritual life. To live without this sorrow for one's sins or distance from God is seen as a dangerous state of indifference.</note> O Lord, how sweet are your ways! But who can walk in them without fear? I fear failing to serve you; and when I labor in your service, I find nothing that satisfies me, because I could do nothing capable of paying even the smallest part of what I owe you. It seems to me that I wish to devote my entire self to serving you. But when I consider attentively the depth of my own misery, I see that I cannot—

<summary>A soul expresses deep spiritual anxiety and humility, lamenting its imperfections and the debt it owes to God while navigating the "tempests" of life. The writer expresses a desire for total devotion but is hindered by a sense of personal inadequacy.</summary>

<meta>This page serves as the legal front matter for a 1655 publication. It provides the "Privilege," or royal copyright authorization, necessary for printing and selling books in 17th-century France. This specific privilege belongs to Robert Arnauld d'Andilly, a prominent scholar associated with the Port-Royal movement.</meta>

->### Extract of the King’s Privilege.<-

BY grace and Privilege of the King, given at Compiègne on the 27th day of August in the year of grace 1652. Permission is granted to Monsieur <note>The original term is "Sieur," a title of respect for a gentleman or official.</note> Arnauld d’Andilly, Counselor to His Majesty in his Councils of State and Privy Council, to have printed the Translation he has made of **Saint John Climacus** <note>John Climacus was a 7th-century Christian monk and author of "The Ladder of Divine Ascent."</note>, and all the Translations he shall make hereafter of the works of the **Holy Fathers of the Church**, and other Ecclesiastical Authors, for the duration of twenty years; with very express prohibitions to all persons, of whatever quality and condition they may be, from printing, selling, or distributing any of it in any place under the obedience of His Majesty <note>meaning anywhere within the French kingdom</note>, without the consent of the said Monsieur d’Andilly, or of those who shall hold his rights, under penalty of confiscation of the copies, of all costs, damages, and interests, and of three thousand livres <term>livres: the primary currency of France before the Franc; 3,000 was a very substantial fine</term> in fines, as is set forth more at length by the said Privilege. Signed, by the King in his Council.

->BERAULD.<-

*And the said Monsieur Arnauld d’Andilly has transferred his right of Privilege for the present Treatise of the **Meditations of Saint Teresa**, to Pierre le Petit, Printer and Bookseller in Ordinary to the King, to enjoy it, following the agreement made between the said Monsieur d’Andilly and him.*

*Finished printing for the first time on the 1st day of March 1655.*

<summary>This page presents the formal royal "Privilege" or copyright granted to the scholar Arnauld d'Andilly, protecting his translations of religious texts from unauthorized reproduction for twenty years. ->## FIRST PETITION.<-

->### FOR MONDAY.<-

**Although** the name of **Father** is the one that best suits all these petitions, and the one that gives us the greatest confidence, and through which the Lord willed to bind himself to give us what we ask of him: nevertheless, we will not act against his arrangement and ordinance by adding other titles, since they belong to him with such truth. Furthermore, through these titles, devotion is awakened, and the fire on the altar of our heart is fanned by renewing its wood <note>The author uses a common Baroque metaphor comparing the spiritual heart to a temple altar, where the "fire" of prayer must be regularly fed with "wood" (holy thoughts or names of God) to keep it burning.</note>. 8      MEDITATIONS

[to] us so favorable.

So that the [spiritual] fire has all of Monday to spend on this name of "Father" and the first petition alone; consider that your Father is God, three in persons and one in essence, the beginning and author of all things, a being without beginning; who is the cause and author of all beings, through whom we move, and in whom we live, and through whom we are <note>original: "por quien nos mouemos, y en quien viuimos, y por quien ſomos." This is a paraphrase of Acts 17:28: "For in him we live, and move, and have our being."</note>, who sustains all, and maintains all. And consider yourself a child of this Father, so powerful that He can create infinite worlds; and so wise, that He will know how to govern all of them just as He knows how to govern this one, without His providence failing any creature from the highest Seraphim <note>The highest order of angels in medieval theology, known for their burning love for God.</note> to the lowest little worm of the earth; so good, that He is always freely communicating Himself to all according to their capacity.

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues a devotional commentary on the Lord's Prayer, specifically focusing on the personal relationship between the believer and God as Father, and the call for all humanity to reflect the divine image.</meta>

### ON THE OUR FATHER. 9 <note>original: "SOBRE EL PATER NOSTER." The "Our Father" is the central prayer of the Christian tradition, also known as the Lord's Prayer.</note>

And especially, let a person consider and say: How good is this Father to me? For he willed that I should have being <note>meaning "existence"</note>, and that I should enjoy this dignity of being his child, while leaving uncreated other men who might have been better than I. Here, one should weigh how much this Father deserves to be loved and served, who out of his goodness alone created all things for me, and created me so that I might serve him and find joy in him.

On such an occasion, he shall ask for light for all people so that they may know Him, and for love so that they may love Him and be thankful for such great blessings; and that all might be such—so virtuous and holy—that the image of God their Father shines within them, and that His paternal name might be glorified and hallowed in everyone, as the name of a Father who has such children that they resemble the Father who created them.

<summary>This page provides a meditation on the gift of existence, encouraging the reader to feel a personal sense of gratitude for being chosen by God as a child. It concludes with a petition for all of humanity to live virtuously so that they may reflect God's nature and bring honor to His name.</summary>
<keywords>Lord's Prayer, Our Father, gratitude, creation, divine image, holiness, spiritual dignity</keywords>

->10 MEDITATIONS<-

Following this, there immediately follows (bringing to mind the many sins of men) a heavy sorrow at seeing such a good Father offended by his ungrateful children; and a rejoicing to see that there are servants of God in whom the holiness of their Father shines forth: feeling sorrow for every sin and bad example one might see; rejoicing likewise at every virtue in whomever one might see or hear of it; giving thanks to God because He created the holy martyrs, confessors <note>In this context, "confessors" refers to saints who suffered for their faith or lived lives of great holiness but were not killed as martyrs.</note>, and virgins, who clearly showed themselves to be children of such a Father.

Let us consider what happens between good subjects <note>The original Spanish uses *vassallos* (vassals), referring to the feudal relationship of loyalty between a person and their sovereign.</note> and their King, and from this, let us lift our thoughts to the King of Heaven. ->### THIRD PETITION<-

->#### FOR WEDNESDAY.<-

The third petition is: **Thy will be done** <note>original: "Hagaſe tu voluntad."</note>: desiring that in all things the will of God may be fulfilled. And we ask even more: that it be fulfilled on earth as it is in heaven, with love and charity. This petition follows the two previous ones very well, for it is such a just cause that the will of the eternal Father should be fulfilled in everything most perfectly by His children, and that of the sovereign King by His subjects <term>vassals: "vassallos" in the original; this reflects the historical context where the relationship between a King and his people served as a metaphor for the relationship between God and humanity</term>.

The Wise Man says: <note>original: "Sabio"; likely referring to the biblical Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 28:1-4, which discusses vengeance and forgiveness</note> How is it possible that man does not forgive his brother, and yet asks forgiveness from God? The subject matter of this petition <note>Refers to "Forgive us our debts as we forgive our debtors"</note> is most general, and embraces infinite things; because the debts are beyond counting, the redemption is most abundant, and the price of forgiveness is infinite, which is the death and passion of Christ.

Here one must recall or bring to memory one’s own sins, and those of the whole world: the gravity of a <term>mortal sin|In Catholic theology, a grave sin committed with full knowledge and consent that separates the soul from God's grace</term>, which, because it is an offense against God, cannot be redeemed or paid for by another: the restoration for so many offenses committed against such great and infinite majesty and goodness. offenses that we commit against Him in return for this, for from all these debts we ask Him to release us, when we ask Him to forgive us our debts. <note>The author explains the Christian view that sins are "debts" owed to God, which only He can cancel.</note> In the execution of this work lie all His riches, and all our good fortune; for He is the one offended, the Redeemer, and the ransom.
<term>ransom</term> <note>In this theological context, "the ransom" (Spanish: *el rescate*) refers to the price paid by Christ—his life—to buy back humanity from the debt of sin.</note>

Let us consider the condition of the doctors of the earth, who do not visit unless they are called, and who visit those who pay them better more often than those who are most in need. -># ON THE LORD'S PRAYER <note>original: "PATER NOSTER"</note>. 61<-

...faith more from the heart.

This way of praying greatly pleases the Lord; for just as great lords delight in hearing the common folk <note>The original Spanish uses "rusticos," referring to uneducated or rural people, to contrast their simple speech with the "composed" words of the elite.</note> who ask them for something bluntly and simply, so the Lord receives much pleasure when we pray to Him with such urgency that—instead of pausing to search for highly composed and orderly words—we say the first ones that come to mind to briefly express our need to Him. 

We see this with Saint Peter and the Apostles when, fearing they would drown, they said: *Lord, save us: for we perish.* And like the Canaanite woman <note>A biblical figure from Matthew 15 who persisted in her prayer for her daughter's healing.</note> when she begged for mercy. And like the Prodigal Son, saying: *Father, I have sinned against heaven and against you.* And like the mother of Samuel <note>Referring to Hannah, whose silent, moving lips in the Temple led the priest Eli to mistake her for being drunk, though she was praying for a son.</note> when she said: *O Lord of hosts <note>original: "Señor de las batallas," literally "Lord of Battles," a traditional rendering of the Hebrew "Yahweh Tzva'ot."</note>, if you will look with favor upon the afflic-*

### I.

**O LIFE**, life, how can you sustain yourself being absent from your life? <note>Teresa often uses "Life" to refer to God, creating a poetic paradox where the soul's physical existence is seen as a kind of death because it is separated from the "True Life" (God).</note> In such solitude in which you occupy yourself? ## XI.

**O**, God help me, oh God help me, what great torment it is for me when I consider what a soul will feel—one that has always been respected here on earth, and loved, and served, and esteemed, and pampered—when, upon finishing the act of dying, it sees itself already lost forever and understands clearly that its state shall have no end. For there, it will be of no use for the soul to try to avoid thinking of the matters of faith, as it has done here; and it sees itself, and sees itself being torn away from what it will seem had not even begun...

## II. MEDITATION.

### How the Soul that loves God deeply finds itself divided between the desire to enjoy him, and the obligation to help one’s neighbor. <note>The "neighbor" (le prochain) refers to the Christian duty of charity toward others, often presented as a tension for those who prefer quiet contemplation.</note>

->AFTER THE COMMUNION. 93<-

...of my God, how different your effects are from those produced by the love of the world! This worldly love wants no company, because it seems to it that company separates it from the person it loves.

But yours, O my God, increases instead the more it sees an increase in the number of those who love you; it feels its joy diminish when it considers that not everyone enjoys such great happiness.

It is for this very reason, O my supreme good, that in the midst of the greatest contentment <note>original: "contentemens," referring to the spiritual satisfaction or peace found in prayer.</note> received with you, the soul is grieved when it reflects on the great number of those who despise these same blessings and who will be eternally deprived of them. ...the sin that we carry, and plunged into this **divine hell** <note>This "divine hell" is a mystical paradox: a state of being so utterly consumed and "lost" in God’s love that the soul has no desire to escape, mirroring the permanence of hell but through the lens of absolute bliss.</note> of divine love, from which one would no longer hope—or to put it better—from which one would no longer fear being able to ever leave. ...offend you. O <term>free will<note>The human capacity to choose between good and evil, often discussed in 17th-century theology as a gift that is only truly "free" when it chooses the good.</note></term>, how you are a slave to your liberty if you are not attached, as if with nails, by love and by the fear of Him who created you. Alas! When will that happy day come when you see yourself engulfed in this infinite sea of sovereign truth, where you will no longer have the liberty to be able to sin, nor will you even wish to have it? For then you will be delivered from all miseries, and happily reunited and, as it were, naturalized <note>In this context, "naturalized" refers to the soul being adopted into the divine nature, becoming a citizen of heaven.</note> with the life of your God, your creator, and your master.

God is blessed because He knows Himself, He loves Himself, and He enjoys Himself, without it being possible for Him to do otherwise. He does not have, nor could He ever have, the liberty to forget Himself or to cease loving Himself; and to do so would not be a perfection in Him.

# ADVICE

### HER NUNS. <note>Saint Teresa of Avila (1515–1582) was a Spanish mystic and reformer of the Carmelite Order. These "advices" or maxims were practical rules for daily living and spiritual growth.</note>

1. **T**HE SPIRIT of man resembles the earth, which, though fertile, nevertheless produces only thorns and briars when it is not cultivated.

2. Speak highly of all spiritual persons: such as <term>religious men</term> <note>In this context, "religieux" refers specifically to members of male religious orders, such as monks or friars.</note>, priests, and hermits.

3. When you are with many others, always speak little.

4. Conduct yourself with great modesty in all things that you do, and in all matters you handle.

[...at once] <note>This is the conclusion of point 5 from the previous page.</note>, especially in matters of little importance.

6. Speak to everyone with a moderate cheerfulness.

7. Never make a mockery of anything whatsoever.

8. Never reprimand anyone except with discretion and humility, and with a secret awareness <note>The original French uses "confuſion ſecrette," referring to a humble, inward recognition of one's own unworthiness or flaws before God.</note> of your own particular failings.

9. Always adapt yourself to the temperament of the people with whom you interact. 10. Never speak without having thought well about it beforehand, and having strongly commended your words to our Lord, so as to say nothing that might be displeasing to Him.

11. Never excuse yourself, unless there is a great reason to do so.
